## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every moving robot → robots should be STILL
- After switch time: score increases for every moving robot → robots should still be STILL... wait, no.
- After switch time: score increases for every robot that IS MOVING → robots should STOP
- Before switch time: score increases for every robot that IS STATIONARY → robots should MOVE

So:
- Phase 1 (before switch): robots must MOVE to minimize score
- Phase 2 (after switch): robots must STOP to minimize score
- The green wall lights up at the switch time → perceiving green (color 2) signals phase 2

The optimal strategy: **Move before switch, Stop after detecting green.**

The champion (6075.78) uses a complex 3-state machine. Score 2 is a simple 1-state Stop (6169.46) which is surprisingly good - this suggests stopping always is nearly optimal, meaning the penalty for moving in phase 2 outweighs the benefit of moving in phase 1, OR the robots aren't effectively moving in phase 1.

Wait - let me reconsider. A pure Stop scores: ~500 steps × 12 robots = 6000 (phase 1 penalty) + 0 (phase 2 penalty) = ~6000. That matches score ~6169.

A pure Move scores: 0 (phase 1) + ~600 steps × 12 robots = 7200 (phase 2 penalty) = ~7200. That matches score ~7182 (the exploration FSM).

So the ideal is: Move in phase 1, Stop in phase 2 when green is detected.

The champion achieves 6075 which is close to pure Stop (6169). The champion has attraction + stop + exploration states. The green color is 2 in the color scheme.

Key insight: Detect green (color 2, `--l<I>x<K> 2`) to trigger stopping.

**My designs:**

1. **Survival**: Champion verbatim
2. **Exploitation 1**: Small tweaks to champion parameters
3. **Exploitation 2**: More tweaks
4. **Simple 2-state**: Explore → Stop on seeing green (color 2)
5. **Simple 2-state**: Stop → Explore (briefly), then stop on green
6. **3-state move/stop with green detection**
7. Various other topologies

The green color in the simulator is color index 2 (from the clr parameter: {1,2,3,4,5,6} where 2=green based on standard ARGoS colors). Let me use color 2 for green detection.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is clear: **2-state FSM with Exploration (rwm=5) → Stop, triggered by seeing green (color 2) with probability 0.95**. This scores 4191.88, far better than pure Stop (6127).

The strategy works perfectly:
- Phase 1: robots explore (move) → score doesn't increase
- Phase 2: when green wall appears, robots detect it and stop → score doesn't increase

Key insights from results:
- Lower rwm (5) beats higher rwm (10, 20, 30) - slower turning is better for exploration
- Single transition (just green detection) beats dual transitions (adding random probability)
- Attraction-based movement is worse than exploration for this task
- The p=0.95 and l=2 (green) are the critical parameters

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try p values near 0.95: 0.99, 0.98, 0.90, 0.85
- Try rwm values near 5: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7, 8
- Try adding a second transition from Stop back to Explore (very low prob) to handle false stops before switch
- Try 3-state variants: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop

**Exploration strategy:**
- Try Repulsion instead of Exploration (spreads robots to cover more area, better chance of detecting green)
- Try 3-state with intermediate state
- Try different transition structures

For the Stop state back-transition: the issue is if robots stop before the switch, they lose points in phase 1. But with p=0.95 and l=2, they only stop when green is visible, so false positives would need there to be a green-colored object before the switch - which there isn't. So the 0.001 back-transition probability is fine (rarely fires).

The key question: can we do better than 4191? The theoretical minimum would be ~0 if all robots move in phase 1 and all stop in phase 2 immediately. With 12 robots, 500 steps average switch time, 700 steps remaining: score ≈ 0 (phase 1) + some (phase 2 if they don't stop fast enough). With p=0.95 detection, they should stop within ~20 steps of seeing green, so score ≈ 12 × 20 = 240 in phase 2. But actual score is 4191, so something is off - perhaps not all robots can see the green wall at once (range-and-bearing limited), or the exploration means they're sometimes stationary anyway.

Actually - maybe robots aren't always moving during exploration (they might slow down or stop momentarily). Lower rwm means less random turning, more straight-line movement - better coverage. Let me try rwm=1 (nearly straight).

Also: what if we use GoToColor green (behavior 8) as the exploration phase? Robots move toward green when they see it... wait, that's the opposite of what we want. Actually in phase 1, we want movement regardless. In phase 2, we want stopping when green appears.

The current champion's state 0 (Exploration with rwm=5) transitions to Stop (state 1) when green is seen. This is correct.

Let me focus exploitation tightly around the champion.

## Reasoning

The pattern is very clear now:
- **Champion**: 2-state FSM, Exploration (rwm=1) → Stop on green (color 2, p=0.95). Score: 3456.71
- rwm=1 and rwm=2 both score the same (3456.71), suggesting very low turning rate is optimal
- The simpler the better: single transition from Explore to Stop on green detection
- Adding extra transitions (neighbor count, random) hurts performance
- Repulsion is worse than exploration

The near-straight-line movement (rwm=1) maximizes coverage and movement during phase 1, while the green detection (p=0.95) ensures rapid stopping in phase 2.

**What can still be improved?**
1. The detection probability p=0.95 - could try 0.99, 1.0 (but 1.0 might cause issues if green is briefly visible before switch... actually no, green only appears at switch time)
2. The back-transition probability 0.001 from Stop - could try 0.0 (but minimum might be 0.001) or keeping it very low
3. rwm=1 is already minimum - can't go lower
4. Could try adding a second condition from Explore state: e.g., also transition on neighbor count being very high (clustered = not exploring well)
5. Could try 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop, to move robots toward green faster
6. Could try p=0.99 or p=0.98 for detection (faster response)

The key insight: rwm=1 means nearly straight movement. The detection p=0.95 means ~95% chance per step of stopping when green is visible. If a robot sees green for multiple steps, it will almost certainly stop quickly.

**Exploitation (7 machines):**
- Champion exact copy
- rwm=1, p=0.99 (faster detection)
- rwm=1, p=0.98
- rwm=2, p=0.99
- rwm=1, p=0.95, back-transition 0.0001 (even lower false restart)
- rwm=1, two transitions: green + another rare trigger
- rwm=2, p=0.95 (same as top-2)

**Exploration (6 machines):**
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop (robots move toward green wall faster)
- 3-state: Explore/Explore(different rwm) → Stop on green
- 2-state with Attraction instead of Exploration (robots cluster, then one sees green and... but can't communicate)
- Try GoToColor(green) directly as first state with stop as second
- 4-state variant

For the 3-state GoToColor approach:
- State 0: Explore (rwm=1), transition to State 1 on seeing green (l=2)
- State 1: GoToColor green (vel=1.0, clr=2), transition to State 2 on... hmm, or just stop
- Actually: State 0: Explore → on green → State 1: Stop. State 1 has no back. Simple.

Or: State 0: Explore → on green → State 1: GoToColor(green). State 1: GoToColor(green) → after some time → State 2: Stop. This is complex and may not help.

Better exploration idea: What if we use GoToColor(green) as the main behavior after detection? That would make robots move TOWARD green (moving = bad in phase 2). So that's wrong.

Actually for phase 2, we want STOP. The champion already does this optimally. Let me focus on tweaking p and rwm values carefully.

One thing to try: what if the Stop state has NO back-transition at all? The minimum for --n<I> is 1 transition, so we can't have 0. But we can make p very small like 0.0001.

Also: what if we try different LED colors for state 0? Probably irrelevant to score.

Let me also think about whether the detection could be improved. With p=0.95 per step, the expected time to detect green (given it's visible) is 1/0.95 ≈ 1.05 steps. With p=0.99 it's ~1.01 steps. The improvement is minimal. The real question is: how quickly can all 12 robots see the green wall? That depends on their positions and the wall location - not something we can control with the FSM much.

One idea: if robots spread out more evenly (repulsion), they'd be more likely to collectively see the green wall quickly. But repulsion scored worse. Maybe a brief repulsion phase then exploration?

Let me try a 3-state: Repulsion → Exploration → Stop, where repulsion spreads robots initially, then they explore, then stop on green. But timing the repulsion→exploration transition is tricky.

Or: use neighbor count to trigger: if too many neighbors (clustered), repel; otherwise explore. On green: stop.

Wait, for 4 states:
- State 0 others=[1,2,3]: D=0→1, D=1→2, D=2→3
- State 1 others=[0,2,3]: D=0→0, D=1→2, D=2→3
- State 2 others=[0,1,3]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→3
- State 3 others=[0,1,2]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→2

For state 0 → state 2 (stop): D=1
For state 1 → state 2 (stop): D=1
For state 2 → state 1 (explore): D=0... wait state 2 others=[0,1,3], so D=0→state0.
For state 2 → state 0 (explore): D=0 ✓
For state 3 → state 2 (stop): D=2

Let me redesign:
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1), → State 2(stop) on green: D=1 (state 0 others=[1,2,3], D=1→state2)
- State 1: Repulse, → State 2(stop) on green: D=1 (state 1 others=[0,2,3], D=1→state2); → State 0 on random: D=0
- State 2: Stop, → State 0 on rare: D=0 (state 2 others=[0,1,3], D=0→state0)
- State 3: unused... I need to use all states. Let me use a 3-state instead.

Actually I don't need to include state 3 if I use --nstates 3.

Let me finalize my 14 machines cleanly without errors:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.99 --l0x0 2 --n0x1... 

No wait, with --n0 1, there's only one transition. Let me be careful.

Machine with n0x1 needs --n0 2. Let me recount:

For --n0x1 to exist, --n0 must be at least 2.

OK let me write all 14 cleanly:

1. Champion (survival): rwm=1, p=0.95, l=2
2. Exploit: rwm=1, p=0.99, l=2

## Reasoning

The champion is: **2-state, Explore(rwm=1) → Stop on green(l=2, p=0.95), Stop back-transition p=1e-04**. Score 3333.90.

Key findings:
- rwm=1 (nearly straight-line movement) is optimal
- p=0.95 for green detection works well
- Very low back-transition (1e-04) is slightly better than 0.001
- The 4-state machine (score 3342.20) with Explore+Repulse states all feeding into Stop on green is nearly as good
- Adding neighbor-count transitions to Explore state causes catastrophic failures (score 5960, 7070)
- p=0.98 with wrong back-transition is bad

**What to try:**
1. Survival: exact champion copy
2. Exploitation: vary p (0.90, 0.92, 0.95, 0.97, 0.99, 1.0) keeping everything else same
3. Exploitation: vary back-transition (1e-05, 5e-05, 1e-04, 5e-04)
4. Exploitation: try rwm=1 with p=0.95 and no back (--p=0.0 not valid, use 1e-05)
5. Exploration: 4-state variant similar to #2 best performer but tweaked
6. Exploration: 3-state dual-explore → Stop
7. Keep transitions SIMPLE - avoid neighbor count conditions (they caused worst performers)

The critical insight: the back-transition from Stop must be VERY small (1e-04 beats 0.001 slightly). The robots might occasionally false-stop? No - green only appears at switch time. The back-transition is irrelevant to score since robots only enter Stop after seeing green (phase 2). So the difference between 1e-04 and 0.001 is just noise, or 1e-04 genuinely keeps robots stopped more reliably after seeing green.

Actually with p_back=0.001, expected steps in Stop before returning to Explore = 1000 steps. With p_back=1e-04, it's 10000 steps. Since the run is 1200 steps total, both are effectively permanent. The difference in score is likely noise.

Let me focus on:
- p detection: 0.90, 0.93, 0.95, 0.97, 0.99, 1.0
- back prob: 1e-04 (champion), 1e-05, 5e-05
- rwm: 1, 2
- Some 3-state and 4-state explorations

**Important**: Avoid adding neighbor-count transitions to the main explore state. Keep it clean.

For the 4-state best performer (3342.20):
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1) → State 2(Stop) on green p=0.95
- State 1: Repulse(3.0) → State 2(Stop) on green p=0.95; → State 0(Explore) on random p=0.005
- State 2: Stop → State 0(Explore) on random p=0.001
- State 3: Explore(rwm=1) → State 2(Stop) on green p=0.95; → State 0 on random p=0.005

This is essentially the same as the 2-state but with redundant states. The key is all paths lead to Stop on green.

Let me tweak this 4-state: reduce rep gain, change random probabilities.

For state destination indices with 4 states:
- State 0 others=[1,2,3]: D=0→1, D=1→2, D=2→3
- State 1 others=[0,2,3]: D=0→0, D=1→2, D=2→3  
- State 2 others=[0,1,3]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→3
- State 3 others=[0,1,2]: D=0→0, D=1→1, D=2→2

In best performer #2: state 0→state 2 = D=1, state 1→state 2 = D=1, state 2→state 0 = D=0, state 3→state 2 = D=1 ✓

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (3333.90) is stable: **rwm=1, p=0.95 detection, back-transition 1e-04**.

Key findings from round 5:
- p=0.90 (3400) and p=0.99 (3400) both slightly worse than p=0.95 - the champion p=0.95 seems optimal
- back-transition: 1e-04 is best; 5e-04 and 0.001 are worse; 0 is also slightly worse (3428 vs 3333)
- The scores are very close (3333-3450 range) - we're near a local optimum
- Multiple structures (2-state, 3-state, 4-state) all converge to similar scores when they use rwm=1 + green detection

The score of 3333 likely represents: ~278 average steps × 12 robots that fail to stop quickly after switch. The bottleneck is probably that not all robots can see the green wall simultaneously (wall is one block, not all robots face it).

**What could help:**
1. Try p values between 0.90-0.99 more finely: 0.91, 0.92, 0.94, 0.95, 0.96
2. Back-transition: 1e-04 is sweet spot; try 2e-04, 5e-05
3. Try detecting other colors (maybe the wall color index isn't 2 but something else? The champion works so color 2 = green is confirmed)
4. Try 2-state with GoToColor(green) as phase 1 behavior - robots actively seek green wall, but this means moving in phase 2... actually no: if in state 0 (GoToColor green) they only move when green is visible, otherwise they might wander. When green appears at switch, they move TOWARD it (still moving = bad). Skip.
5. Try Attraction as phase 1: clusters robots, then one detects green and stops. But clustered robots all face same direction... might help or hurt.
6. Key insight: maybe we need robots to be more SPREAD OUT so more of them can see the green wall. Repulsion could help spread them. But repulsion-first attempts scored ~4454 (bad). The issue was the timing of repulsion→explore transition.

Actually, re-reading the 4-state best performer: it has Repulse state with random 0.005 back-transition to Explore, meaning robots spend most time in Explore (200 steps average in Repulse). That's a lot of time repulsing. Maybe a shorter repulsion phase would be better.

**The fundamental limit**: robots can't communicate the green detection to each other. Each robot must independently see the green wall. With rwm=1 (straight movement), robots quickly cover the arena and maintain spread.

**New ideas for exploration:**
- Use LED signaling: when a robot sees green and stops, it turns on a bright LED. Nearby robots see the LED and also stop (via C=7 detecting that LED color). This cascade effect could spread the stop signal!
  - State 0: Explore(rwm=1), LED=off. On green → State 1 (Stop).
  - State 1: Stop, LED=yellow(4). On seeing yellow(4) robots → stay in Stop (already there).
  - But other robots in State 0: if they see yellow LED (from stopped robot) AND transition on l=4... they'd go to Stop too!
  
  This is the cascade idea:
  - State 0: Explore, cle=0. Transition 1: on green(l=2) → Stop. Transition 2: on yellow(l=4) → Stop.
  - State 1: Stop, cle=4 (yellow LED). Rare back transition.
  
  This way: first robot sees green → stops → emits yellow. Nearby robots see yellow → also stop. Chain reaction!

This is a genuinely new and potentially powerful idea. Let me design this carefully.

FSM 2-state cascade:
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1), cle=0. 
  - Transition 0: D=0→State1 on green(l=2) p=0.95
  - Transition 1: D=0→State1 on yellow(l=4) p=0.95
- State 1: Stop, cle=4(yellow).
  - Transition 0: D=0→State0 on random p=1e-04

But with 2 states and 2 transitions from state 0: --n0 2, --n0x0 0 (→state1), --n0x1 0 (→state1). Both point to same destination (state 1), different conditions. This should work!

Actually wait - with 2 states, from state 0, D can only be 0 (pointing to state 1). Both transitions go to state 1. That's valid.

Let me also try cascade with magenta (5) since that's a different color from yellow:
- State 0: Explore, cle=0. On green → Stop. On magenta → Stop.
- State 1: Stop, cle=5 (magenta).

And try with cyan (6):
- State 0: Explore, cle=0. On green → Stop. On cyan → Stop.  
- State 1: Stop, cle=6 (cyan).

These cascade machines could significantly improve performance if robots are within 0.69m range of each other.

**Plan for 14 machines:**
1. Champion (survival): rwm=1, p=0.95, l=2, back=1e-04
2. Exploit: p=0.94
3. Exploit: p=0.96
4. Exploit: p=0.92
5. Exploit: p=0.95, back=2e-04
6. Exploit: p=0.95, back=5e-05
7. New: 2-state cascade with yellow LED (green OR yellow → stop, stop emits yellow)
8. New: 2-state cascade with magenta LED
9. New: 2-state cascade with cyan LED
10. New: 3-state cascade (green → intermediate → stop)
11. Exploit: 4-state similar to best performer #4 but with p=0.95
12. Exploit: 3-state similar to best performer #3 but with p=0.95
13. New: 2-state cascade with green LED on stop (robots signal they stopped with green)
14. Exploit: p=0.91

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.95 --l0x0 2 --s1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 1e-04

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Massive breakthrough!** The cascade idea works spectacularly: score dropped from 3333 to **191.51** — a 17x improvement!

The champion mechanism:
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1), detect green(l=2, p=0.99) OR detect yellow LED from stopped robots(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, emit yellow LED (cle=4), rare back-transition (1e-04)

This cascade works: one robot sees green wall → stops → emits yellow → nearby robots detect yellow → also stop → emit yellow → chain reaction propagates through the swarm!

**Key findings:**
- p=0.99 beats p=0.95 for both conditions
- Yellow (l=4), magenta (l=5), and cyan (l=6) all work (scores 191-193), yellow is best
- Single-transition (no cascade) scores ~3333 — the cascade is the key innovation
- Back-transition 2e-04 is worse than 1e-04; 1e-04 is optimal

**What to try now:**
1. Try p=1.0 for both transitions (maximum responsiveness)
2. Try different LED colors for cascade: already tested yellow(4), magenta(5), cyan(6) all ~193; yellow+green? 
3. Try adding a THIRD condition to state 0: also react to a third color (triple cascade)
4. Try 3-state cascade: green → stop AND nearby stopped robot → stop, with intermediate state
5. Try varying back-transition: 1e-05, 5e-05
6. Try adding cascade detection to stop state too (to stay stopped more reliably)
7. Try p=0.999 
8. Try detecting TWO cascade colors (yellow AND magenta) in state 0 for 3 total transitions

**For the triple-cascade (3 transitions from explore):**
- On green(l=2, p=0.99) → Stop
- On yellow(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop  
- On magenta(l=5, p=0.99) → Stop
- Stop emits yellow

Or stop emits both yellow and... wait, each state has ONE LED color. But the cascade works via the LED of the stopped robots.

**Key question:** Can we make the cascade faster/more reliable?
- Higher p values (0.999, 1.0) would help
- Detecting multiple stop-LED colors would help if different robots emit different colors

Actually: if ALL stopped robots emit yellow (cle=4), then any exploring robot within 0.69m of a stopped robot will detect yellow and also stop. The cascade speed depends on the robot density and range.

**3-transition explore state:**
- Detect green(l=2, p=0.99) → Stop
- Detect yellow(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop (cascade from yellow-LED stopped robots)
- Detect magenta(l=5, p=0.99) → Stop (if we have another stop state emitting magenta)

But with one stop state, all stopped robots emit yellow. So the 3rd transition detecting magenta would never fire (no magenta sources). Unless we use it as a different LED for state 0 exploring robots?

Actually - the LED color of state 0 (exploring) is cle=0 (off). If we change it to emit a color, then stopped robots AND moving robots would emit colors, which would confuse the cascade.

Better idea: **two stop states with different LEDs**, both reachable from explore:
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1), cle=0. On green→Stop1. On yellow→Stop1. On magenta→Stop2.
- State 1: Stop, cle=4(yellow). Back rare.
- State 2: Stop, cle=5(magenta). Back rare.

But this splits the cascade signal... not obviously better.

**Simpler improvement: p=1.0 for both detections**

Let me focus tightly on exploitation of the champion plus a few exploration ideas:

**14 machines plan (actually 13 this round):**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. p=1.0 for both (green and yellow detection)
3. p=0.999 for both
4. p=0.99 green, p=1.0 yellow (faster cascade spread)
5. p=1.0 green, p=0.99 yellow
6. back=1e-05 (even more stable stop)
7. back=5e-05
8. Add 3rd transition: also detect magenta(l=5) → Stop (in case some robots stopped with magenta LED)
9. 3-state: two explore states + stop (more robots exploring, cascade still works)
10. Cascade with magenta LED (p=0.99 both conditions) - variant of #3/#4 best
11. Cascade with cyan LED (p=0.99 both) - variant
12. Cascade with yellow, p=0.99/0.99, back=1e-04, but rwm=2 (slightly more turning)
13. Triple cascade: detect green, yellow, AND cyan in explore; stop emits yellow

For machine 8 (triple cascade, 3 transitions):
- --n0 3
- Transition 0: green(l=2, p=0.99) → Stop
- Transition 1: yellow(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop
- Transition 2: magenta(l=5, p=0.99) → Stop
- Stop emits yellow (cle=4)

For machine 13 (triple cascade):
- Detect green, yellow, cyan → Stop
- Stop emits yellow

Let me write these carefully:

**Machine 3 (triple cascade):**
```
--nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 3 
  --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.99 --l0x0 2  (green → stop)
  --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --p0x1 0.99 --l0x1 4  (yellow → stop)
  --n0x2 0 --c0x2 7 --p0x2 0.99 --l0x2 5  (magenta → stop)
--s1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 1e-04
```

With 2 states, from state 0: D must be 0 (→ state 1). All three transitions use D=0. ✓
From state 1: D must be 0 (→ state 0). ✓

**Machine with 4 transitions (detect green + 3 LED colors):**
```
--n0 4
  green(l=2), yellow(l=4), magenta(l=5), cyan(l=6) → stop
```
Max transitions is 4, so this uses all 4.

Let me finalize all 13:

# ROUND 8/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion: 170.49** - The key insight is that the back-transition from Stop should be **p=0** (robots never leave Stop once they enter it). This beats 1e-04.

Current champion: rwm=1, detect green(l=2, p=0.99) OR yellow(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop(cle=4/yellow), back=0.

The cascade works: robot sees green → stops → emits yellow → nearby robots detect yellow → stop → emit yellow → chain reaction.

**Key findings:**
- p=0 back-transition (truly permanent stop) beats 1e-04
- p=0.99 beats p=1.0 and p=0.999 slightly (noise or genuine)
- Yellow LED (l=4) cascade is best; cyan (l=6) is slightly worse
- Triple-cascade (adding magenta) tied with double at 171.88

**What to optimize now:**
1. Try p=0 back-transition with other p values (0.999, 1.0) for detection
2. Try different detection probabilities with p=0 back: 0.95, 0.999, 1.0
3. Try 3-state cascade: Explore → on green/yellow → intermediate state → Stop (faster propagation?)
4. Try 4-transition explore: detect green + yellow + magenta + cyan all → Stop
5. Try adding cascade detection of MORE colors (3 transitions: green, yellow, magenta)
6. Try p=0 back with triple cascade

The score of 170 means ~14 robot-steps of movement in phase 2 across all 12 robots (170/12 ≈ 14 steps). This is nearly optimal - robots stop within about 1-2 steps of the switch on average.

**Can we get lower?** The theoretical minimum is ~0 if all robots stop instantly when green appears. With p=0.99 detection per step, a robot seeing green stops after ~1 step. The cascade adds maybe 1-2 more steps for distant robots. So ~170 might be very close to the floor.

**Ideas to push lower:**
- p=1.0 detection (instant stop when green/yellow visible) but it scored 171.88 vs 170.49 - might be noise
- More cascade colors to spread faster
- Try detecting ALL available LED colors in state 0 (4 transitions: green wall + 3 LED colors)

**4-transition explore state:**
- Detect green(l=2, p=0.99) → Stop
- Detect yellow(l=4, p=0.99) → Stop
- Detect magenta(l=5, p=0.99) → Stop  
- Detect cyan(l=6, p=0.99) → Stop
- Stop emits yellow

This way any robot that was emitting ANY non-zero LED color would trigger stops. But only stopped robots emit yellow (state 1, cle=4). So detecting magenta/cyan from state 0 would only fire if there were other robots emitting those colors - which there aren't in this design. So 4-transition with yellow only meaningful cascade is just 2 transitions effectively.

Unless... we make a 3-state design where:
- State 0: Explore, cle=0 (no LED). Detect green → State 2(Stop). Detect yellow → State 2(Stop).
- State 1: Explore, cle=4(yellow LED). Detect green → State 2(Stop). Detect yellow → State 2(Stop).  
- State 2: Stop, cle=4(yellow). Back p=0.

But this doesn't help because exploring robots in State 0 emit no LED, and robots in State 1 would emit yellow while still moving - causing false cascade triggers!

Actually... wait. What if we have a more complex cascade:
- Exploring robots emit NO LED (cle=0)
- First robot to see green → enters Stop, emits yellow
- Nearby robots detect yellow → enter Stop, emit yellow
- This is already what the champion does

**Better idea:** What if the Stop state emits TWO signals? It can't (one LED per state). But we could have two Stop states:
- State 0: Explore(rwm=1), cle=0. On green(p=0.99) → State 2. On yellow(p=0.99) → State 2. On magenta(p=0.99) → State 2.
- State 1: Stop, cle=5(magenta). On yellow(p=0.99) → State 2. Back p=0.
- State 2: Stop, cle=4(yellow). Back p=0.

Wait, with 3 states and state 0:
- others=[1,2]: D=0→state1, D=1→state2

This is getting complex. Let me simplify: focus on exploiting what works.

**Plan for 13 machines:**
1. Champion (exact copy) - p=0 back, p=0.99 both detections, yellow
2. p=1.0 both, back=0
3. p=0.999 both, back=0
4. p=0.99 green, p=1.0 yellow, back=0
5. p=1.0 green, p=0.99 yellow, back=0
6. Triple cascade (green+yellow+magenta), p=0.99, back=0
7. Triple cascade, p=1.0, back=0
8. 4-cascade (green+yellow+magenta+cyan), p=0.99, back=0
9. p=0.99 both, back=0, magenta LED (instead of yellow)
10. p=0.99 both, back=0, cyan LED
11. Triple cascade p=0.99, back=0, magenta stop LED
12. p=0.99 green, p=0.99 yellow, back=0, rwm=2 (slight variation)
13. p=0.95 both, back=0 (compare to back=1e-04 which scored 193)

All machines use 2-state structure with D=0 from state 0 (only other state is 1).

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.99 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --p0x1 0.99 --l0x1 4 --s1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (170.49) is stable at p=0.99/0.99, back=0. This round's best performers all score ~184, which is worse than the champion's 170.49 - suggesting the champion result was a favorable random seed, and we're near the true optimum.

**Key observations:**
- p=0.99/0.99 with back=0 is the champion
- p=1.0/0.99 scores 184 (worse!) - very counterintuitive
- p=0.999/0.999 scores 184.86 (worse than 0.99)
- p=0.95/0.95 with back=0 scores 185 (worse)
- Triple and quad cascade (adding magenta, cyan) score same as double (184.12)
- The scores are tightly clustered around 184 this round; the 170.49 champion may be near the stochastic floor

**What's happening:** The scores ~184 this round vs 170 champion suggests high variance between runs. We're likely at/near the optimum for this design space.

**Strategy for round 9:**
- Keep champion exactly
- Try very fine p variations around 0.99: 0.97, 0.98, 0.99, 0.995
- The fact that p=0.99 > p=1.0 suggests there might be a sweet spot slightly below 1.0
- Try different combinations: p_green ≠ p_yellow
- Try adding a second detection from Stop state (if robot in stop sees NO yellow, it might re-explore - but back=0 prevents this)
- Explore: 3-state designs where robots can communicate more
- Try: what if exploring robots emit a color that OTHER exploring robots react to, creating movement coordination?

Actually, let me think about what could genuinely improve beyond 170:

The bottleneck is robots that are FAR from the green wall and from any stopped robot. They keep moving in phase 2. The cascade propagates at walking speed (roughly one robot-to-robot connection per step). With 12 robots in a bounded arena, the cascade should complete in a few steps.

**New ideas:**
1. **Faster cascade**: What if exploring robots emit a "searching" LED color, and stopped robots react to it? No - stopped robots have back=0.
2. **Pre-positioning**: What if robots cluster near walls (attraction to walls? not available). GoToColor on some existing color?
3. **Alternative colors**: What if the green wall is actually a different color index? The champion works well with l=2, so green=2 is confirmed.
4. **Multi-hop cascade improvement**: Add more cascade colors so the signal spreads faster. But results show triple/quad cascade = same score as double.

The scores are very tightly clustered. Let me focus on:
- Fine-grained p variations (0.97, 0.98, 0.985, 0.99, 0.993, 0.995)  
- Back-transition variations (0, 1e-05)
- Different LED colors for the cascade (already tried yellow=4, magenta=5, cyan=6 - all similar)
- Mixed p values: p_green=0.99, p_yellow=0.98 etc.

Since we only have 2 rounds left, I should concentrate resources on the most promising variants.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. The champion (170.49) has held for 3 rounds. The scores are very tightly clustered (170-183 range), suggesting we're near the stochastic optimum.

**Key trends:**
- p=0.995 scores 180.12 (this round's best, worse than champion's 170.49)
- p=0.993 scores 180.26
- p=0.99 scores 182.54 this round vs 170.49 champion - high variance, same machine!
- The champion score of 170.49 appears to be a lucky run; the true mean is ~182

**What the data tells us:**
- p values 0.99-0.995 are all very close in performance
- The topology (2-state cascade, yellow LED, back=0) is confirmed optimal
- Triple/quad cascade doesn't help (same score as double)
- Lower p (0.97, 0.98) is slightly worse

**For the final round, I should:**
1. Keep the champion (survival)
2. Run multiple copies of the champion to get a better estimate and possibly beat 170.49 by luck
3. Try p=0.994, p=0.996, p=0.997, p=0.998 (fine grid around 0.993-0.999)
4. Try a few genuinely different structures in case there's a hidden optimum

The key insight: since scores are stochastic and the champion's 170.49 may be near the floor, submitting multiple copies of the same champion machine gives the best chance of finding a run that beats it. However, the optimizer likely runs each machine multiple times and averages, so copies don't help directly.

Let me try the full range around p=0.993-0.999 with back=0, plus a few variations:

**12 machines:**
1. Champion (exact)
2. p=0.994 both
3. p=0.996 both
4. p=0.997 both
5. p=0.998 both
6. p=0.995 both (best this round)
7. p=0.993 both (2nd best this round)
8. p=0.995 green, p=0.99 yellow (asymmetric)
9. p=0.99 green, p=0.995 yellow (asymmetric)
10. Triple cascade (green+yellow+magenta), p=0.995, back=0
11. 4-cascade, p=0.995, back=0
12. p=0.995 both, back=1e-05 (tiny back vs 0)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 12 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.69 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
